=== FILE: Box Deadlift Tutorial： Stronger Hamstrings, Better Deadlift [6tccLhGrq94].txt ===

 Hey, everybody, what's up? Chad Wessley-Smith here for Juggernaut Training Systems, and today I'm going to be continuing our How, Why, When series, talking about when you use different exercise variations, how to do them, why to do them, when to use them to make your squat bench and deadlift stronger. Today, I'm going to be talking about the box deadlift. As always, videos here on the Juggernaut Training Systems channel are brought to you by the Juggernaut AI app. So if you want training that's totally customized for you, looking at your gender, age, height, weight, strength, experience, where you miss your lifts, all that type of stuff, get a two-week free trial of the Juggernaut AI app, and it takes all those things into consideration to make a totally customized program for you that adapts to your feedback. So you can visit the link down in the description or go to juggernautai.app for that two-week free trial. Alright, so the box deadlift is a deadlift variation, more of an accessory type exercise, but that was taught to me by Mikhail Koklyov and Boris Cheko at a seminar that we hosted with them back in 2014, I think maybe 2015, but let's say 10-ish years ago, they were out here doing a seminar tour and they showed us this exercise and it made a great impact on my own deadlift training and a lot of the guys that I coached. So the box deadlift is really an RDL variation that gives you a tactile cue of a box or bench or something pressing up against your calves to help you create a very vertical shin angle and maximize the tension that you create in your hamstrings while really learning how to hinge and create that kind of ideal deadlift position. So how do we do it? I've got Playa Box here. Make sure you've got something braced up against it. I got a kettlebell sitting on the ground behind it so it doesn't slide back. You can use a bench. There used to be a device called a vert pole that you could use. You could set up a bar low in the rack and have your calves pressed up against that. Lots of ways to do it. One's not really better than the others. But what we want to do is stand so we can press our calves back against it and create a vertical or even negative shin angle in doing an RDL. So I'm pressing my calves back. It's going to be this high hip position. And as I do my RDL, I've got to keep my calves in constant contact with the box. And if I was to allow my calves to come off of the box, that's going to be kind of failing to do the exercise correctly. So it's got to be that constant contact. I'm going to be pushing my knees back, almost leaning back against it a little bit. And you're going to get way, way more tension in your hamstring by doing it this way. For someone like me who struggled with their deadlift position, a common problem for people of advanced size. This was a great exercise for me to learn what it really felt like to load the hamstrings to hinge better. So the execution of the exercise is pretty simple. You're just keeping your calves pressed into the box, pressed against the bench, throughout the entirety of the movement. Now this means you might not be able to go all the way down to the floor. So an incline clapping push-up, depending on your level of readiness, is going to depend on the box height that you want to use. It's just going to be a little bit easier to do it on the incline than it would be with your hands on the ground. An assisted variation, and this is going to come up later as a more advanced, actually accelerated version, but being used for different reasons. The assisted version is going to be if you aren't strong enough, relatively strong enough to do these on the ground. the assisted version. You're going to kind of tuck yourself through here, band underneath your armpits. It's just going to give me a little bit of a boost, or if the clapping's too much, just pop your hands off the ground. So the incline clapping push-ups or band assisted clapping push-ups are going to be a great introduction to this explosive upper body work. You're going to want to look at maybe a three or four week block and just increase either your number of reps per set or your total number of sets. So over the course of three weeks, that might be like four sets of three, four sets of four, four sets of five reps, or, you know, staying at three reps the whole time, go four sets of three, five sets of three, six sets of three. So you're building up some capacity and getting ready for our next progression. And this next progression we're going to use is very similar to, again, to a lower body plyometric progression, because these are jumps for the upper body. So we're looking at jumping up, jumping down, and then jumping down and up. So the progression we use for this, and if you've used the Juggernaut BJJ app, read the Juggernaut football manual, these are going to be very familiar to you. We're doing what we call box push-ups, which is like a box jump with your arms. And we're doing drop push-ups, which is like an altitude landing. And we're doing depth push-ups, which is like a depth jump where we're jumping down and up. So box push-ups, we're going to start on the ground in between two boxes. And just like box jumps, the height of the box isn't that important. How high you project your center of mass is much more important. So in the box push-ups, I'm starting here in between, and I can do a couple different types of starts. I can do a more static start, or a dynamic start. Either way, I'm trying to land as high as I can on the boxes with soft hands. I don't want to be slapping the top of the boxes and catching myself low, because it's not a contest of how quickly I can pull my hands up. Just like a box jump shouldn't be a test of how well you can pull your knees to your ears. So you're going to use this for the first part of the progression. Typically, I'll do this, four sets of two, four sets of three, four sets of four, or four sets of two, five sets of two, six sets of two in a three-week wave, and then deload. With these box push-ups, you could also get into variations a little bit more specific for the bench press, or maybe you're wearing a weighted vest, you pause at the bottom and explode up from there. Then you can create a progression with heavier and heavier vests from week to week, or higher and higher boxes with the same weight vest. But we need to be overloading it in some way. So that's the first part of our progression. The next part is going to be drop push-ups. Drop push-ups, we start on top, we jump down, we stick the landing, we're trying to decelerate as quickly as possible. So a common mistake that I see with the drop push-ups is people land high and kind of decelerate themselves softly down to the bottom position. I'm trying to get to the bottom position as quickly as possible and stop as quickly as possible. So that's going to be our second step in the progression, drop push-ups. Hard, quick deceleration about halfway down in the push-up position. You can manipulate this with the height of the boxes. So you could change that from week to week, or just use that same loading strategy like four sets, two, five sets, two, six sets, two, if you're at the same height boxes, or four sets, two, four sets, three, four sets, a four. Finally, the third step in our progression are depth push-ups. Now we're kind of taking the drop push-ups and box push-ups and putting them together into a depth jump for the upper body. This is the more advanced variation. So now we're starting on top, jumping down, jumping back up. So we're trying to have a short ground contact time, get off the ground quickly, get as high as we can. Again, soft hands, cat-like, ninja-like hands throughout all of these. So that's a progression that we can use over nine to 12 weeks. Three weeks of box push-ups, deload. Three weeks of drop push-ups, deload. Three weeks of depth push-ups, deload. After that, you could get into band accelerated push-ups, which are going to be just like the band assisted push-ups I showed you earlier. But now, instead of doing them for someone who's not strong enough and ready enough to do clapping push-ups on their own, now we're using the band assistance, more in like a French contrast type of method to help you get off the ground even quicker, reach even higher weights. So that's going to be a bit more of an advanced variation. But again, it's the exact same exercise, just being used for a different purpose. So all these explosive push-up variations are great. There's also a ton of good explosive upper body training that you can do with a medicine ball. So let's take a look at that. Another great option for the explosive upper body training is using medicine ball throws. It uses a similar type of progression with that. Going from a static start to a dynamic start. So that would be the equivalent of the static start box push-ups, dynamic start box push-ups. Then just catching the heavy ball would be the equivalent of the drop push-ups and the depth push-ups where you drop and throw. You've got to be trying to throw it as high as possible. Of course, you can manipulate this with either the height of the drop or the weight of the ball. Hopefully you learned a little something about using explosive upper body training to benefit your bench press or to improve sport performance for athletes like offensive and defensive linemen, shot putters, combat sport athletes. A lot of great benefits because jumps aren't just for the lower body, they're for the upper body as well. Alright, so part of your training on the Juggernaut AI app may be the safety squat bar. So, how do you do it? Luckily for you, safety squat bar is, you know, from about here down, exactly the same as regular squatting, maybe even a little bit simpler. And it's gonna be a little bit simpler because of the slight camber to the safety squat bar. And this is a Rep Fitness safety squat bar, great one, one of my favorites that I've used. And that slight camber is gonna put the weight a little bit in front of your body, and kind of give you a bit of a counterbalance, similarly to a front squat, but without the awkward ways that you might have to hold the front squat. So it's a very natural position for you to just put your hands on the handle. So pretty much everything about safety squat bar is the same as a regular squat from a breathing bracing standpoint. Foot positioning, you know, big toe pressure, knees and hips together, all that stuff that you can see in dozens, maybe hundreds, of other Juggernaut Training Systems YouTube videos. The only thing to note is that when you're doing the safety squat bar, because of that slight camber, you want to avoid pushing up or pulling down on the handles, all right? You want to let the bar stay as neutral as possible. So you might have to squeeze the handles hard, but if you push up or pull down on the handles, you're gonna kind of change the leverage of the weight and sort of take away some of the function that you might be using this bar for, to address specific weak points. So this little bit of camber to the bar is what's going to make the safety squat bar my favorite, really the only specialty bar that I ever tend to recommend for the squat. And because of that camber and the way that it positions your upper back, it's going to really help you avoid or train against your upper back getting rounded over in the squat. So if you feel like you lose upper back position, if you get pushed forward in the squat and start looking like this as you go, losing the upper back position, not just your hips rising faster than your shoulders, then the safety squat bar can be a great option for you. The other roles that it's going to play is if your shoulders, elbows, wrists are beat up from a particularly low bar squatting, then the safety squat bar is going to be a great option because it's going to take all the stress away in those situations. That also makes it a great option for hypertrophy training or any kind of high bench press frequency or volume phase. We'll talk about that a bit more. And the safety squat bar is going to be great for athletes if they have shoulder problems, even if they don't have shoulder problems, because as I mentioned, it tends to be a much more natural squat pattern than a high bar, low bar barbell back squat because of the slight camber and the counterbalance that that creates. So how to do it? Simple, just like a regular squat. Just don't push up or pull down on the handles. You're going to have to really actively engage your upper back so you can learn to keep that stable and tall throughout the squat. Why to do it? If you're rounding over in the squat, you need to build up that upper back strength. Or if you're struggling with arm pain, elbow pain, shoulder pain from low bar squatting particularly. Or for athletes, the barbell back squat isn't part of their competition. So non-barbell sport athletes. This can be a great option if it's simpler for them to learn, if it's easier for them to execute. You can also use the safety squat bar, of course, just for variation, for variation's sake a little bit to avoid adaptive resistance. Now, when do you want to do the safety squat bar? You can use it in any phase of training. In a hypertrophy phase, this could be the primary squat variation that you're using because it's loaded very similar to a high bar squat or a front squat, but much simpler to execute from a technical standpoint than a front squat and going to allow you to go heavier than a front squat would typically. If you're bench pressing a lot frequently or having arm pain during those higher volume phases, then the safety squat bar is going to be a great way for you to not have your squat and bench training interfere with each other. And then during a hypertrophy or strength phase, it's going to be a great secondary option to address the weak point of rounding over a little bit if you're kind of on the weaker quads side of things. As long as you're forcing yourself to stay upright with the safety squat bar, you could let the safety squat turn into like this real squat morning, rounded over, lift it with your back type of move. And if you're already a stronger back relative to leg strength, quad strength, specifically squatter, then that's probably not going to be a great option for you. But if you elevate your heels, push your knees forward, force yourself to keep an upright torso and really stress your quads with it, then it can be really useful for you in hypertrophy or strength phases. And then finally, in peaking, safety squat bars, main role in peaking would be kind of as a fatigue management tool to avoid your arms feeling too beat up to interfere with your bench press. So if you're someone like I've been in the past where your elbows or shoulders are killing you, maybe, you know, ulna nerve type of issues, the inside of the elbow, from heavy bench pressing and heavy low bar squatting as you get closer to a powerlifting competition, then maybe make your secondary squat day each week. The safety squat bar, just, you know, keep in mind at that point, while it is a squat and it's very similar to high bar and low bar squatting, it's not quite the same. So when we're in a peaking phase and we're looking to develop technical prowess in our competition lifts, you're making a little bit of a trade-off for arm health and still getting good, similar squat stimulus. But if you don't have the arm pain, I'd rather someone be doing, you know, two-time-a-week competition squatting or a comp squat and a high bar squat is going to be a little bit similar, but we're really splitting hairs at that point. So hopefully now you know a little bit better how to do the safety squat bar, why to use them in your training, and when to use them in your training. So if you're new to deadlifting, that's with your feet close together. Not like you'll see a lot of people complaining about on the internet with your feet wide apart, which is called sumo deadlifting. So a simple way to find your stance in the conventional deadlift is to do a vertical jump. Is that as high as you can jump? You want to take the stance in which you jump as high as you can jump. There we go. When I say jump, you say high. And that foot position is going to be where we start your deadlift stance from. Now, as you get more advanced and changes happen with your training, you might widen it out a little bit. You might narrow it down a little bit, but this is a simple place to start. Depending on your hip mobility, you could go from toes straight like Marissa has right now to toes very slightly out, maybe 15, 20 degrees turned out if you find that you have a hard time getting down to the bar. But easy place to start where you do a vertical jump from. If you're having to use, let's say, 25 pound plates, smaller metal ones, you want to elevate the bar on blocks. So it's at its standard height about nine and a half inches. So as you set up to address the bar, you want to be close enough to the bar that it's over the laces in your shoes. So we don't want it out way over the toes and we don't want it so close that when you're standing upright, it's touching your shins. Because if Marissa was to have the bar touching her shins as she goes down to grab it, now it's going to push the bar away. If the bar is out over the toes too much, now the bar is going to be too far away from you. It's going to make it feel a lot heavier and be a lot more stressful on your back. So now that we've found your stance, vertical jump with bar over the start of the shoelaces. Now that could change a little bit, you know, if you wear adult size shoes, size 14s like me, or little kid size shoes, two and a half like Marissa. The next part of progressing in learning the deadlift is learning how to hinge. The deadlift is a hinge movement at the knee and hip together. It's not a squat. So it's a hip dominant movement. So there's going to be very little movement of the knee relative to the angle in the hip. So learning how to hinge is very, very important. As you think about, you know, if you're watching this, you're new to lifting, you're thinking, what is a hip hinge? All right. A simple way to start doing that exercise is the way that, you know, if you've played basketball before and you get tired and you end up in this position, all right, that's going to be how you hinge. So if you put your hands on the front of your thighs, all right, and then just slide them down to your knees. So I'm pushing my butt back, feeling my hamstring stretch, keeping my hand in contact with the leg the whole time. That's going to create a hip hinge. That should get you close enough to the bar where you can get your fingers, fingertips on it. From that point, you want to reach down, grab the bar, and then use the bar to pull you down into position. So we're trying to create as vertical a shin as possible with your shoulders slightly like an inch in front of the bar. So if we draw a straight line here, the bar is coming right through Marissa's upper back. So again, it is not a squat. If Marissa was to squat down to the bar, see how their shins come forward, it pushes the bar away from her, and you might feel like, oh, if I get my butt down more, I'm going to be able to use my legs a lot more in the deadlift, but it doesn't really end up working out that way. Because if you try and squat down to the bar, then when you initiate the lift, your hips are going to rise faster than your shoulders. And this is a very common mistake you see with beginner lifters all the way up to advanced lifters. They're trying to squat the bar off the floor, and they lose that tension in their hamstrings. So again, we're creating a hip hinge, pushing our hips back, sliding our hand down the thigh, kind of finding that middle school basketball, bent over, grabbing your shorts, tired position. Use the bar to pull yourself into position, and that is our start position. Bar is through the upper back, eyes forward. We could be looking slightly at the ground. We don't want to be trying to look up at the ceiling, putting ourselves in a ton of extension. Now, how wide should her hands be? You want your hands just outside your shins. All right? If you go too wide, you're adding a lot of extra distance. It's going to be hard to create tension in the lats. If you go too close and your hands are kind of in front of your knees, now you're pushing the bar out away from you. Now, there are a different ways to grab the bar. You could use what's called a double overhand grip. All right? Or you can use the much stronger over-under grip. So if you're right-handed, it's probably that your right hand is the overhand, where you're looking at the back of the hand, and your left hand is the underhand. All right? Where you're just turning it. That's going to help you have a much, much stronger grip. If you don't feel comfortable with that and you're brand new to deadlifting, it's probably not a big deal to use a double overhand grip for a while because the weights that you're going to be using aren't going to be challenging your grip that much. I wouldn't get into the habit if you have an aspiration to be a competitive powerlifer at some point. I wouldn't start wearing straps right off the bat. So Spoto Press is very much like a normal bench press with two key distinctions. You want to try and bring the bar down very quickly, almost drop it. And then you're going to stop it right off of your chest, maybe half an inch to one inch above the chest. So you're not going to have the stability of the bar resting on your chest. You're going to have to create that stability all through your lats, arms, and shoulders to be able to control the bar path. How to do it? Pretty simple. Normal bench press setup. You could do wide grips photo, close grips photo, regular grips photo, whatever works for you. All of those are available to you. And you're trying to bring the bar down quickly, almost like you're dropping it. So Spoto Press is an exercise that can be used for a lot of different purposes, but our main one that we find it helps people improve is their mid-range strength in the bench press. So kind of that in-between area of, is it the off the chest until you feel it turn over into the lockout? Depending on your arm length, this could be different distances off the chest, but let's call it probably two to six or seven inches off the chest is going to be the mid-range for a lot of people. You might have really short arms and mid-range might start at an inch for you. But once you've driven the bar off the chest and you feel it, you know, kind of just turn into the next phase, that middle phase of the bench press. I think the Spoto Press does a great job with this because it eliminates some ability for you to create leg drive to initiate the press because the bar is not touching your chest. You can't really transfer the energy from the legs through the body into the bar. So you're having to build that much more strength in the arms, shoulders, stability in the lats and control of the bar. And that ability to control the bar as it hovers translate to you being able to keep it through a better bar path and just have overall better control and particularly build up your shoulder strength, which has a big influence on the mid-range strength. When would you want to use this in your training? This can really be used in any phase of training. We'll have people do Spoto Press during hypertrophy. Definitely want to de-emphasize competition bench press in hypertrophy to avoid adaptive resistance. So if someone's struggling with mid-range, weak point will probably, they'll probably be doing Spoto Press at least once a week during this time, but they might use other mid-range exercises like incline bench press as well. Then you can use it in a strength phase or peaking phase as a complement to your competition bench press training. So that could mean that it has its own day, a secondary or tertiary day of bench pressing throughout the week, or you're doing it as a secondary or tertiary exercise. So you could do a competition bench press for a top set, maybe a down set or two, and then switch to the Spoto Press to kind of complement that. Or it could be on its own day. And again, that could be a secondary exercise for the day, or sometimes we'll have people, you know, if they're benching maybe two or three times a week, a day that's kind of just variations, and it's going to serve some fatigue management purposes in there as well, because range of motion is a little bit smaller. It'll vary pretty widely, like I'm much weaker in the Spoto Press than the competition bench press. My best Spoto is probably 87, 88% of my best comp bench, while Marissa is just as strong or stronger in the Spoto Press. And a lot of that has to do with kind of bench press style. If you bring the bar down faster and kind of let it sink into your chest a little bit, you might struggle a lot more with the strictness and control of the Spoto Press. So you can get a two-week free trial of Juggernaut AI at the link down in our description. All right, so the trap bar deadlift. How do you do it? So we do have a longer, more in-depth video just about trap bar deadlift technique that you can go check out. I think it's called how to trap bar deadlift, but the condensed version of it is. Lots of different trap bars out there. We have a rep open trap bar, which is nice because it has its little built-in jacks there, so it's easy to put the weight on and off. But main thing with the trap bar deadlift is you need to get a line so the bar is through your midfoot. And you want to make sure that the bar is balanced in your hand so that you're putting your hand right, you know, in the middle of the handle so it doesn't tip forward or tip backwards on you. So trap bar deadlift is kind of halfway between a squat and a deadlift, and you can kind of manipulate your hip position with it a higher, more hinged position or a lower, squattier type of position. But just like with any other deadlift variation, we want the bar through the midfoot. We're pushing the ground away with our full foot, taking big air into our belly to create 360 degrees of pressure through your abs, and you're protecting your armpits like someone's trying to come tickle them, squeezing your triceps into your lats to create a rigid torso position. Now, why would you want to include the trap bar deadlift in your training? As a power lifter, trap bar deadlift has a useful but limited role. So because it is kind of blurring the line between squat and deadlift, it can be useful to address weakness off of the floor, and it could be something that you want to include if you're struggling with maybe some low back pain where conventional deadlifts are bothering you, but trap bar deadlifts feel okay. If you're just trying to build kind of grip and upper back strength up or in very general phases of training like a bridge block that we use in Juggernaut AI or as a hypertrophy exercise because it does let you use quite a bit of load. The transfer to the conventional deadlift, I think, is a little dubious for some people, but it can help address a weakness off the floor by teaching you how to push with your legs better to initiate the lift. Now, when would you want to include it into your training? It's probably best included in very general phases of training like bridge block or an off-season type of program or earliest phases like hypertrophy. Using the trap bar deadlift can aid with adaptive resistance or avoiding adaptive resistance if you're doing, you know, just all conventional deadlifts all the time or all similar deadlifts all the time. Trap bar deadlift, it's still a deadlift. It's still, you know, similar enough that it's going to have transfer but will be a novel stimulus to help you avoid accommodating too much to the type of training that you've been using. For non-powerlifters, for non-strength athletes, but for, you know, football players or MMA fighters or, you know, sprinters or whoever else who'll be watching this video, trap bar deadlift can have a lot of application for them and can really be used at any point in the training. It might be the primary lower body exercise that some of these athletes use because it's very simple to execute. A bit simpler and, you know, some would say safer technique than the conventional deadlift, you're able to get a lot of stimulus from it. I mean, you can go really heavy and, you know, athletes are in the weight room to learn how to produce force. So exercises that you can put a lot of weight onto the bar with are going to be a great option to give you that both muscular and neural force production qualities that they're chasing. And then you can manipulate it through different loading strategies so it can fit into pretty much any phase of the athlete's training or within the context of their training plan. And those are the three main criteria that we're looking for when choosing exercises for athletes is can they do it, you know, safely? Can they produce output on it? And how does it fit into the context of the rest of their training plan? So for strength athletes, a little bit of a limited application, you know, off-season phases, maybe hypertrophy for some people, but for non-strength athletes can use it, you know, widely throughout all kinds of different phases of the year. You could do it heavy, you could do it for speed, you know, put a VBT device on, do it with bands, all kinds of different things and loading strategies to get the stimulus that you're looking for with athletes. So now hopefully you understand a little bit better how to do the trap bar deadlift and check out that longer video we have on trap bar deadlift technique, why to use it in your training and when to include it into your training.
